Uncertainty quantification for Markov chain induced martingales with application to temporal difference learning
Weichen Wu, Yuting Wei, Alessandro Rinaldo

Abstract
We establish novel and general high-dimensional concentration inequalities and Berry-Esseen bounds for vector-valued martingales induced by Markov chains. We apply these results to analyze the performance of the Temporal Difference (TD) learning algorithm with linear function approximations, a widely used method for policy evaluation in Reinforcement Learning (RL), obtaining a sharp high-probability consistency guarantee that matches the asymptotic variance up to logarithmic factors. Furthermore, we establish an O ⁡ ( T − 1 4 ​ log ⁡ T ) O(T^{-\frac{1}{4}}\log T) distributional convergence rate for the Gaussian approximation of the TD estimator, measured in convex distance. Our martingale bounds are of broad applicability, and our analysis of TD learning provides new insights into statistical inference for RL algorithms, bridging gaps between classical stochastic approximation theory and modern RL applications.



1
Introduction

Markov chains are important tools in statistical machine learning for modeling dependent data.
They provide a theoretical framework for analyzing sequential algorithms, are fundamental to MCMC sampling, Hidden Markov Models, and Reinforcement Learning (RL), and are widely used in a multitude of high-stakes applications, including NLP, finance, biology, and AI systems.

Given their widespread use, it is crucial to provide rigorous probabilistic guarantees on the convergence, stability, and error bounds of Markovian sequences. Indeed, uncertainty quantification is essential for assessing the reliability, robustness, and generalization power of machine learning models built on Markov chains. For example, in MCMC sampling, precise uncertainty quantification ensures reliable convergence diagnostics and variance estimation, preventing misleading inferences in Bayesian models. In RL, uncertainty estimation for value functions helps balance exploration and exploitation, leading to more stable decision-making. Similarly, understanding uncertainty in reverse stochastic processes improves sample quality and diversity in diffusion-based generative models.

In the first part of the paper, we derive novel concentration and high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for a certain type of bounded vector-valued martingale difference sequences induced by (not necessarily reversible) Markov chains, and illustrate the broad applicability of this data dependency structure. While there is a rich literature on concentration bounds for vector- and matrix-valued additive functionals of Markov chains
(
Garg et al., 2018
;
Qiu et al., 2020
;
Neeman et al., 2024
)
, we are in fact not aware of any high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds for data of this form. Recently,
Bong et al. (2023)
have studied high-dimensional Gaussian approximations for
m
-dependent sequences over the class of hyper-rectangles, though those bounds and methods do not directly apply to martingale sequences.

In the second part of the paper, we turn to the study of uncertainty quantification for the temporal difference (TD) learning algorithm, a widely used method for value function estimation in RL
(
Sutton and Barto, 2018
)
, assuming Markovian data. TD learning is an instance of stochastic approximation
(
Robbins and Monro, 1951
)
, designed to solve fixed-point equations via randomized approximations of residuals. In recent years, largely motivated by the diffusion and success of RL applications, there have been significant advancements in statistical inference techniques for Markov chain-based algorithms in RL; see, e.g.,
Bhandari et al. (2021)
;
Mou et al. (2020)
;
Fan et al. (2021)
;
Li et al. (2021)
;
Samsonov et al. (2024b)
;
Samsonov et al. (2024a)
;
Srikant (2025)
. In this paper we obtain novel and sharp bounds that improve on the current state of the art.

Summary of the contributions

We make two types of theoretical contributions. First, we derive new high-dimensional finite sample approximations to Markov data and martingale processes in discrete time that are of broad applicability. Specifically,

•

In Section
2
, we introduce the notion of
Markov chain induced martingales
, which we argue are naturally suited to analyze the concentration and Gaussian approximation properties of additive functionals of Markov chain data. Then in Section
3.1
, we obtain a Bernstein-type inequality for bounded Markov chain induced martingales in Theorem
3.1
, as well as provide a high-dimensional Gaussian approximation bound measured by convex distance in Theorem
3.2
. To the best of our knowledge, both results are novel.

•

In Section
3.2
, we present new concentration and Gaussian approximation bounds for bounded martingale difference sequences that we require to handle Markov chain induced martingales and may be of independent interest. In particular, in Theorem
3.3
and Corollary
3.4
, we obtain novel high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bounds in Wasserstein distance for normalized sums of discrete time vector-valued martingales with deterministic variance exhibiting dependence on the sample size
n
of order
O(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log n)
. We establish this bound by refining and extending a proof strategy recently put forward by
Srikant (2025)
.

Secondly, we establish rates of consistency and distributional approximations to the output of the TD learning algorithm (with Polyak-Ruppert averaging and polynomially vanishing step-sizes), arguably the most popular methodology for policy evaluation using linear approximations in reinforcement learning, with Markovian sequences of length
T
. Specifically,

•

Theorem
4.2
gives a high-probability bound on the Euclidean norm of the estimation error of the TD learning algorithm featuring a sample dependence of order
T^{-1/2}
, up to log factors, and an optimal dependence on the asymptotic variance;

•

Theorem
4.3
provides a high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for the TD estimator in the convex distance with a sample dependence of order
O(T^{-\frac{1}{4}}\log T)
.

Both results are novel and do not have direct counterparts in the RL literature, which has, for the most part, focused on independent samples; see Section
4
for a discussion of relevant work.

Notation

Throughout the paper, we use boldface small letters to denote vectors and boldface capital letters to denote matrices. For any vector
\bm{x}
, we let
\|\bm{x}\|_{2}
be its
L_{2}
norm; for any matrix
\bm{M}
,
\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{M})
denotes its trace,
\mathsf{det}(\bm{M})
its determinant,
\|\bm{M}\|
its spectral norm (i.e., the largest singular value), and
\|\bm{M}\|_{\mathsf{F}}
its Frobenius norm, i.e.,
\|\bm{M}\|_{\mathsf{F}}=\sqrt{\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{M}^{\top}\bm{M})}
. For any
\bm{M}\in\mathbb{S}^{d\times d}
, the set of all
d\times d
real symmetric matrices, we use
\lambda_{\max}(\bm{M})=\lambda_{1}(\bm{M})\geq\lambda_{2}(\bm{M})\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{d}(\bm{M})=\lambda_{\min}(\bm{M})
to indicate its eigenvalues.
For sequences
\{f_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{g_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
of numbers, we write
f_{t}\lesssim g_{t}
, or
f_{t}=O(g_{t})
, to signify that there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that
f_{t}\leq Cg_{t}
for all
t
. We will use the notation
\widetilde{C}
to express a quantity independent of
T
, the number of iterations/sample size, but possibly dependent on other problem-related parameters.

For two measures
\mu,\nu
on the same measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F})
, if
\nu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu
, we use
\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}
to denote its Radon–Nykodym derivative with respect to
\mu
. For any measurable function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
and any number
p>1
, we set
\|f\|_{\mu,p}^{p}:=\int_{x\in\mathcal{X}}|f(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
.
This definition can also be extended to the case of
p=\infty
, by defining
\|f\|_{\mu,\infty}=\text{ess}\sup|f|,
the essential supremum of
|f|
with respect to
\mu
. To simplify the notation, when
p=2
, we write
\|f\|_{\mu}:=\|f\|_{\mu,2}
.

We will consider the following measure of distance between two probability distributions
P,Q
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
: the
total variation distance

d_{\mathsf{TV}(P,Q)}:=\sup_{\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{B}_{d}}|P(\mathcal{A})-Q(\mathcal{A})|
, where
\mathscr{B}_{d}
represents the class of all Borel sets in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; the
convex distance

d_{\mathsf{C}}(P,Q):=\sup_{\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{C}_{d}}|P(\mathcal{A})-Q(\mathcal{A})|
, where
\mathscr{C}_{d}
represents the class of all convex subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; and the
Wasserstein distance

d_{\mathsf{W}}(P,Q):=\sup_{h\in\mathsf{Lip}_{1}}|\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x}\sim P}[h(\bm{x})]-\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x}\sim Q}[h(\bm{x})]|
, where
\mathsf{Lip}_{1}
represents the class of all 1-Lipschitz functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
.

2
Markov chain induced martingales

We study high-dimensional concentration and Gaussian approximations for certain martingale difference sequences that are naturally associated with Markovian sequences, which here we refer to as
Markov chain induced martingales,
defined next. Although our results provide novel tools of broad applicability to the analysis of Markov chain data, they are especially useful in TD learning modeling; see Section
4
.
Below, we briefly introduce the problem setting and main assumptions and formalize the notion of Markov chain induced martingales.

Throughout this paper, we consider a discrete-time Markov chain
\{s_{t}\}_{t=0,1,2,\ldots}
on some (sufficiently well-behaved) state space
\mathcal{S}
satisfying the following assumptions. We refer the reader to Appendix
A.1
for background and details on the quantities introduced below.

Assumption 2.1
.

1.

The transition kernel
P
admits a
unique stationary distribution
, denoted as
\mu
.
1
1

1

It can be guaranteed that any positive recurrent, irreducible and aperiodic Markov chain fulfills this property.

2.

The transition kernel
P
has a
spectral gap

1-\lambda>0
.

3.

The initial state
s_{0}
is drawn from a probability distribution
\nu
that is absolutely continuous with respect to the stationary distribution
\mu
; furthermore, there exists
p\in(1,\infty]
, such that
\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}<\infty
, where
\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}
is the Radon–Nykodym derivative of
\nu
with respect to
\mu
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mu,p}
denotes the
L_{p}(\mu)
norm.

Letting
q\in[1,\infty)
denote the conjugate of
p
, i.e.
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
when
p<\infty
and
q=1
if
p=\infty
, we say that
\nu

satisfies Assumption
2.1
with parameters
(p,q)
if these conditions hold true.

Remark 2.2
.

In our analysis, we do
not
require the Markov chain to be reversible.

We will study functions
\bm{f}:\mathcal{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
of Markov chains that satisfy the following condition.

Assumption 2.3
.

For every
s\in\mathcal{S}
,
\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid s)}\bm{f}(s,s^{\prime})=\bm{0}
and
\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s)}[\|\bm{f}(s,s^{\prime})\|_{2}^{2}]<\infty
.

In this paper, we work with
bounded
measurable functions
\bm{f}
, which automatically satisfy the second moment condition in the above assumption.
It is immediate to see that, for a sequence of functions (possibly the same one)
\{\bm{f}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
each satisfying Assumption
2.3
and a random sequence
\{s_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
drawn from the Markov chain, the
d
-dimensional random vectors

\bm{f}_{1}(s_{0},s_{1}),\bm{f}_{2}(s_{1},s_{2}),\ldots,\bm{f}_{n}(s_{n-1},s_{n})

(1)

form a martingale difference sequence with respect to the natural filtration of the data
\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{f_{i}}(s_{i-1},s_{i})|\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]=\bm{0}\quad\text{for all }i=1,\ldots,n.

We call this type of martingale
a Markov chain induced martingale.

We remark that Markov chain induced martingales are well-known quantities that arise naturally when studying concentration properties and Gaussian approximations for additive functionals of discrete Markov chains.
In detail, suppose that we are interested in analyzing the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{g}(s_{i}),\qquad\text{for }\bm{g}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d},

(2)

where the random sequence
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a draw from a Markov chain and
\bm{g}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu}\mathbb{E}[\bm{g}(s)]=\bm{0}
. We recall that the initial state
s_{0}
of the Markov chain need not be a draw from the stationary distribution. A widely used approach
(
Glynn and Infanger, 2024
;
Makowski and Shwartz, 2002
;
Li et al., 2023
;
Srikant, 2025
)
is to relate the above sum to a sum of terms from a martingale difference via
the Poisson equation

\displaystyle\bm{g}(s)=\bm{U}(s)-\mathcal{P}\bm{U}(s),\quad s\in\mathcal{S},

(3)

holding for
\mu
-almost all
s\in\mathcal{S}
,
where the operator
\mathcal{P}
maps the function
\bm{U}\colon\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the function
s\in\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{P}\bm{U}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid s)}[\bm{U}(s^{\prime})]
; see Appendix
A.1
for details.
Though there can be multiple solutions to the Poisson equation, it is well known that if the function
s\in\mathcal{S}\mapsto\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{g}(s)
is integrable with respect to the stationary distribution, then it is a solution
\bm{U}
to (
3
); see, e.g.,
Douc et al. (2018)
. This is indeed the approach we will follow in Section
4
when we analyze the properties of the TD learning algorithm.

Given a solution
\bm{U}
to the Poisson equation, the partial sum (
2
) can then be expanded as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{g}(s_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\bm{U}(s_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{i+1})\mid s_{i}]\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\bm{U}(s_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{i})\mid s_{i-1}]\}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{i})\mid s_{i-1}]-\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{i+1})\mid s_{i}]\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\bm{U}(s_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{i})\mid s_{i-1}]\}+\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{1})\mid s_{0}]-\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{n+1})\mid s_{n}]

(4)

where the second identity follows from a reordering of the summands and the last line from telescoping. Thus, by defining

\displaystyle\bm{f}(s,s^{\prime}):=\bm{U}(s^{\prime})-\mathcal{P}\bm{U}(s),\quad s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},

we can easily verify that
\bm{f}
satisfies Assumption (
2.3
) and that the first term of (
4
) can be represented as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\bm{U}(s_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\bm{U}(s_{i})\mid s_{i-1}]\}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{f}(s_{i-1},s_{i}),

which is a sum of the terms forming a Markov chain induced martingale (
1
). Thus, the task of establishing concentration and Gaussian approximation properties of the sum (
2
) largely reduces to that of analyzing the sum of a martingale difference sequence. This is the main task we undertake in the first half of the paper.

3
Main results

In this section, we describe our main results.
In Section
3.1
we present a novel Bernstein-style concentration inequality and a new Berry–Esseen bound over the class of convex sets in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
for Markov chain induced martingales. Section
3.2
presents general Gaussian approximation and concentration bounds for vector-valued martingales (not necessarily Markov chain induced martingales) that are needed in our analysis and that may be of independent interest.

3.1
Bounds for Markov chain induced martingales

In our first result, we derive a novel Bernstein-style concentration inequality for the Euclidean norm of an average of a bounded Markov chain induced martingale (
1
), assuming the settings and conditions introduced in Section
2
. We denote with

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu,s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid s)}\Big[\bm{f}_{i}(s,s^{\prime})\bm{f}_{i}^{\top}(s,s^{\prime})\Big]

(5)

the average variance of the martingale initialized from the stationary distribution (i.e. when
s_{0}\sim\mu
),
which is well defined provided that
\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu,s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot|s)}[\|\bm{f}_{i}(s,s^{\prime})\|_{2}^{2}]<\infty
for all
i
.

Theorem 3.1

(Bernstein’s inequality for Markov chain induced martingales)
.

Consider a Markov chain
\{s_{i}\}_{i=0,1,\ldots,n}
satisfying Assumption
2.1
and let
\{\bm{f}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
be a sequence of functions as in Assumption
2.3
such that

\displaystyle\|\bm{f}_{i}(s,s^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq F,\quad\forall i\in[n],\quad\text{and}\quad s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},

for some constant
F>0
.
Then for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{f}_{i}(s_{i-1},s_{i})\right\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{n})}{n}\log\frac{1}{\delta}}+\frac{\sqrt{q}F}{(1-\lambda)^{\frac{1}{4}}n^{\frac{3}{4}}}\log^{\frac{3}{4}}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}\right)+\frac{F}{n}\log\frac{1}{\delta},

(6)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

The proof of this result is provided in Appendix
B.4
.
To establish Theorem
3.1
, we deploy a version of Freedman’s inequality for martingales in Hilbert spaces due to
Peng et al. (2024)
(see
A.20
in the appendix), yielding an upper bound that is dependent on the
conditional
covariance matrix

\displaystyle\overline{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\bm{f}_{i}\bm{f}_{i}^{\top}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}],

where
\{\mathcal{F}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is the natural filtration induced by the Markov chain.
However, since this quantity is not measurable with respect to the trivial
\sigma
-field
\mathscr{F}_{0}
,
we further control the difference between the traces of
\overline{\bm{\Sigma}}_{n}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{n}
using a one-dimensional Hoeffding’s inequality for Markov chains by
Fan et al. (2021)
(Theorem
A.21
in the appendix), which leads to the first two terms in the upper bound (
6
). Notice that, assuming for simplicity that only
n
and
d
vary in the bound, the second and third terms on the right hand side of (
6
) both converge faster than
n^{-1/2}
, so Theorem
3.1
shows that the sample mean of the sequence
\{\bm{f}_{i}\}
converges to
\bm{0}
in Euclidean norm at a rate determined by
\sqrt{\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{n})/n}
with high probability, just like in the i.i.d. case. This bound plays a key role in
our analysis of TD learning in Section
4
and can be potentially used to understand the concentration of other machine learning algorithms.

In our next result, we establish a high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for bounded Markov chain induced martingales in the convex distance, which is amenable to constructing confidence regions/sets, a task that would be otherwise difficult in other metrics, e.g the Wasserstein distance.

Theorem 3.2

(High-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound on martingales generated from Markov chains)
.

Consider a Markov chain
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1,\ldots,n}
satisfying Assumption
2.1
with parameters
(p,q)
and let
\{\bm{f}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
be a sequence of functions as in Assumption
2.3
such that

\|\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{f}_{i}(s,s^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq M_{i}\leq M
for all
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
and
i\in[n]
. Then, letting

\displaystyle\overline{M}=\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{4}}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}},

it holds that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{C}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{f}_{i}(s_{i-1},s_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\{\overline{M}\left(\frac{q}{1-\lambda}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}d^{\frac{3}{4}}\log^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(d\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}\right)+\sqrt{M}d^{\frac{5}{8}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}d\right\}\frac{\log n}{n^{\frac{1}{4}}}.

(7)

To demonstrate how the Berry–Esseen bound in Theorem
3.2
scales with the dimension and with other problem-related quantities, consider the scenario in which

\bm{f}_{1}=\bm{f}_{2}=\ldots=\bm{f}_{n}=\bm{f}
, and
M_{1}=M_{2}=\ldots=M_{n}=M
. Then, in this case,

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim\mu}\Big[\bm{f}_{i}(s_{i-1},s_{i})\bm{f}_{i}^{\top}(s_{i-1},s_{i})\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu,s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid s)}[\bm{f}(s,s^{\prime})\bm{f}^{\top}(s,s^{\prime})]=:\bm{\Sigma},

and
\overline{M}=M
. Therefore, (
7
) implies that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{C}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{f}(s_{i-1},s_{i}),\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma})\right)\lesssim\left(\frac{q}{1-\lambda}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\log^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(d\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}\right)Md^{\frac{3}{4}}n^{-\frac{1}{4}}\log n.

We remark that the dependence on
d
appears both explicitly in the
d^{\frac{3}{4}}
term, and implicitly in
M
term.

The proof of Theorem
3.2
, presented in Appendix
B.5
, relies on arguments used by
Röllin (2018)
to derive Gaussian approximations for univariate martingales, which can be traced back to
Dvoretzky (1972)
. In the recent literature on high-dimensional statistics, this type of technique has been extended to the multivariate setting in

Cattaneo et al. (2022, Lemma B.8)
and
Belloni and Oliveira (2018, Theorem 2.1)
.
Specifically, we construct an auxiliary martingale with deterministic final quadratic variation, i.e. satisfying (
8
) below, and then apply a novel high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for martingale difference sequences in Wasserstein distance whose proof borrows heavily from the recent work by
Srikant (2025)
; see Theorem
3.3
and Corollary
3.4
below. The difference between the target martingale and this auxiliary martingale is handled using a novel Hoeffding inequality for Markov chain, given in Theorem
3.5
. Finally, we combine these bounds using the properties of Gaussian distributions, as well as the relationship between convex distance and Wasserstein distance. These supportive Gaussian approximation and concentration results are novel and non-trivial, and may be of independent interest. They are presented, along with detailed commentary and comparisons with the existing literature, in the next section, which may be read as a stand-alone portion of the paper.

3.2
General vector-valued martingale bounds

This section collects several auxiliary results needed for the proof of Theorem
3.2
, which may be of independent interest. The following theorem provides a novel high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for vector-valued martingales with
deterministic terminal quadratic variation
in terms of the Wasserstein distance. See Appendix
B.2
for the proof.

Theorem 3.3

(Berry–Esseen bound on vector-valued martingales)
.

Let
\{\bm{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a martingale difference process in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to the filtration
\{\mathscr{F}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
.
For every
i\in[n]
, define

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{i}:=\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mid\mathscr{F}_{i-1}],\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{P}_{i}:=\sum_{j=i}^{n}\bm{V}_{i}.

Furthermore, define

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mid\mathscr{F}_{0}],

and assume that

\displaystyle\bm{P}_{1}=n\bm{\Sigma}_{n}\quad\text{almost surely.}

(8)

Then for any
d
-dimensional symmetric positive semi-definite matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{W}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{x}_{i},\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(2+\log(d\|(n\bm{\Sigma}_{n}+\bm{\Sigma})\|))^{+}}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\|(\bm{P}_{i}+\bm{\Sigma})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{x}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|\bm{x}_{i}\|_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\mathsf{Tr}(\log(n\bm{\Sigma}_{n}+\bm{\Sigma}))-\log(\bm{\Sigma}))\right]+\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma})}{n}}.

(9)

Let us compare this result with analogous ones in the literature. Theorem
3.3
may be be regarded as a multivariate generalization of the univariate bound of Theorem 2.1 of
Röllin (2018)
. When
d=1
, Theorem
3.3
agrees with Theorem 2.1 of
Röllin (2018)
, aside from logarithmic factors; indeed, translating our notation into that of
Röllin (2018)
, we have that

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{n}=s_{n}^{2}/n,\quad\bm{P}_{i}=\rho_{i}^{2},\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\Sigma}=a^{2}.

Theorem
3.3
may be compared with Theorem 1 of
Anastasiou et al. (2019)
, offering point-wise Gaussian approximation bounds for twice-differentiable functions of multivariate martingale sequences with deterministic terminal quadratic variation, and with Theorem 2.1 in
Belloni and Oliveira (2018)
, which focuses on thrice-differentiable functions. In contrast, our bound delivers uniform guarantees, as it establishes convergence rates in the Wasserstein distance, which in turn has direct relation with the convex distance (see, e.g., Theorem
A.18
in the appendix).

As alluded above, our strategy to prove Theorem
3.3
closely follows the strategy put forward recently by
Srikant (2025)
, which deploys Stein’s method and Lindeberg swapping. We point out that approach by addressing a gap in the proof arising when condition (
8
) does not hold and by deriving a tighter bound on the smoothness of the solution to the multivariate Stein’s equation, which may be of independent interest (see Proposition
B.3
in Appendix
B.2
and compare it to Proposition 2.2 and 2.3 in
Gallouët et al. (2018)
).

Under an additional technical condition, the bound of Theorem
3.3
can be further tightened, as illustrated in the next result, which is more directly applicable to constructing the Berry–Esseen bound for Markov chain-induced martingales, e.g., Theorem
3.2
. The proof is in
Appendix
B.3
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Under the settings of Theorem
3.3
, further assume there exists a uniform constant
M>0
, such that for any matrix
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and any
i\in[n]
, it satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\bm{Ax}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\|\bm{x}_{i}\|_{2}\bigg|\mathscr{F}_{i-1}\right]\leq M\mathbb{E}\left[\|\bm{Ax}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg|\mathscr{F}_{i-1}\right].

(10)

Then,

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{W}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{x}_{i},\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{n})\right)\lesssim\left[M(2+\log(dn\|\bm{\Sigma}_{n}\|))^{+}+1\right]\frac{d\log n}{\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{n})}{n}}.

The above results bear similarities with Corollary 2.3 in
Röllin (2018)
in the univariate case and Corollary 3 in
Anastasiou et al. (2019)
, valid in multivariate settings (but for a different metric). In establishing our bound, we have lifted the requirement on the conditional third momentum of
\bm{x}_{i}
and fixed a flaw in the proof of Corollary 3 of
Anastasiou et al. (2019)
.

Importantly, both Theorem
3.3
and Corollary
3.4
require a deterministic terminal quadratic variation
\bm{P}_{1}
; see Equation (
8
). We remark that this condition, while impractical, is fairly standard in the literature: see, e.g.,
Bolthausen (1982)
;
Haeusler (1988)
;
Dung et al. (2014)
;
Machkouri and Ouchti (2007)
;
Röllin (2018)
;
Anastasiou et al. (2019)
;
Kojevnikov and Song (2022a)
.

Recently, generalizing arguments from
Belloni and Oliveira (2018)
,
Cattaneo et al. (2022)
derived Berry–Esseen bounds for vector-valued martingales through a generalization of Yurinskii’s coupling that does not require this condition. Specifically,
Cattaneo et al. (2022)
showed that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{c}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{x}_{i},\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\inf_{\eta>0}\left\{\frac{(\beta_{2,2}d)^{\frac{1}{3}}}{\sqrt{n}\eta}+\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\|\bm{P}_{1}-n\bm{\Sigma}_{n}\|\cdot d}{n\eta^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\eta\sqrt{\|\bm{\Sigma}_{n}^{-1}\|_{\mathsf{F}}}\right\},

(11)

with

\beta_{2,2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{x}_{i}\|_{2}^{3}+\|\bm{V}_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{z}_{i}\|_{2}^{3}].

Here,
\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2},\ldots,\bm{z}_{n}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables independent of
\mathscr{F}_{n}
and
n\bm{\Sigma}_{n}
an arbitrary deterministic positive semi-definite matrix. The rate of convergence is no faster than
O(n^{-\frac{1}{12}})
and thus slower than the ones we obtain in Theorem
3.2
; see the discussion paragraph after Appendix
B.3
for details.

For the case of a martingale sequence induced by a Markov chain, in Theorem
3.2
we are able to relax the deterministic quadratic variation condition in (
8
) via a novel high probability bound on the spectral norm of the difference between
\bm{P}_{1}
and
n\bm{\Sigma}_{n}
. This is the content of our the next result.

Theorem 3.5

(Matrix Hoeffding’s inequality for Markov Chains)
.

Consider a Markov chain
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
satisfying Assumption
2.1
with parameters
(p,q)
. Let
\{\bm{F}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
be a sequence of matrix-valued functions from the state space
\mathcal{S}
into
\mathbb{S}^{d\times d}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu}[\bm{F}_{i}(s)]=\bm{0}
and
\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\|\bm{F}_{i}(s)\|\leq M_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
and positive constants
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right\|\geq\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2d^{2-\frac{\pi}{4}}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}\exp\left\{-\frac{1-\lambda}{20q}\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right)^{2}\frac{n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{n}M_{k}^{2}}\right\}.

(12)

Soon after the initial posting of this paper, we became aware of a recent paper by
Neeman et al. (2024)
who in their Theorem 2.5 already obtained the same bound as in Theorem
3.5
. Our proof techniques share some similarities with theirs, as we both make use of the multi-matrix Golden-Thompson inequality of
Garg et al. (2018)
. Although we no longer claim novelty for Theorem
3.5
, we include our proof in Appendix
B.1
for completeness.

The bound of Theorem
3.5
can be more conveniently re-stated as follows: for every
\delta\in(0,1)
, it can be guaranteed with probability
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right\|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{q}{1-\lambda}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}M_{j}^{2}}{n}\log\Big(\frac{d}{\delta}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}\Big)}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}.

(13)

4
Application to TD learning with linear function approximation

In the second part of the paper, we apply our results to study the properties of the TD learning algorithm with linear function approximation under Markovian samples in the context of reinforcement learning (RL). This important subject has been extensively explored in the recent theoretical literature. These results can be broadly classified into two categories: (i) non-asymptotic bounds on the discrepancy between the algorithm’s output and the target quantity, and (ii) non-asymptotic distributional guarantees, such as Berry–Esseen bounds, which measure how fast the sequence of estimators converges to the limiting distribution. However, when it comes to providing tight, non-asymptotic characterizations of general functions of Markov chains — an essential task in analyzing and calibrating machine learning procedures – existing theoretical tools have limited scope and applicability, at least compared to the tools available for handling independent data.
The bounds on the performance and Gaussian approximations of the output of the TD learning methodology are, to the best of our knowledge, the sharpest to date.

We begin by providing some background on TD learning and refer the reader to
Sutton and Barto (2018)
for an accessible yet comprehensive introduction to this topic.

4.1
TD learning with linear function approximation

Consider a Markov Reward Process (MRP), where a Markov chain on a state space
\mathcal{S}
is associated with a
reward function

r:\mathcal{S}\to[0,1]
that maps each state to a reward.
We observe a sequence of state-reward pairs,

\displaystyle(s_{0},r_{0}),\ldots(s_{t},r_{t}),\ldots

where, for each
t
,
r_{t}=r(s_{t})
and
s_{t+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{t})
.
The task of
value function
evaluation concerns with estimating a function
V:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
, where for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
,

\displaystyle V(s):=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}r(s_{t})\Bigg].

Here,
\gamma\in[0,1)
denotes a discounted factor of future rewards towards the current state.
In practice, the state space
\mathcal{S}
used to describe the environment’s configuration is often prohibitively large, making it necessary to consider various approximations of
V
. The most simple and tractable form of approximation is the linear function approximation, which has received considerable attention in literature; see, e.g.,
Bhandari et al. (2021)
;
Patil et al. (2023)
;
Dalal et al. (2018)
;
Li et al. (2024)
;
Samsonov et al. (2024b)
;
Samsonov et al. (2024a)
;
Wu et al. (2024)
.
Specifically, the value function
V
of a policy is approximated by the linear function

\displaystyle V_{\bm{\theta}}(s):={\bm{\phi}}(s)^{\top}{\bm{\theta}},\qquad\quad s\in\mathcal{S},

(14)

for a set of feature maps
\bm{\phi}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a linear coefficient vector
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Throughout the paper, we assume that
\|\bm{\phi}(s)\|_{2}\leq 1
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
Recall that we use
\mu
to denote the stationary distribution of the Markov chain.
The optimal coefficient vector, denoted as
\bm{\theta}^{\star}
, is defined by the projected Bellman equation
(
Tsitsiklis and Van Roy, 1997
)
, which admits the fixed point equation

\displaystyle\bm{A}\bm{\theta}^{\star}=\bm{b},

(15)

where

\displaystyle\bm{A}:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{s\sim\mu,s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid s)}\Big[\bm{\phi}(s)\left(\bm{\phi}(s)-\gamma\bm{\phi}(s^{\prime})\right)^{\top}\Big]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d},\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{b}:=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\limits_{s\sim\mu}\Big[\bm{\phi}(s)r(s)\Big]\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(16)

Below we indicate
\bm{\Sigma}
as the feature Gram matrix, i.e.

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}:=\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu}\Big[\bm{\phi}(s)\bm{\phi}^{\top}(s)\Big],

(17)

and use
\lambda_{\Sigma}
and
\lambda_{0}
to denote its largest and smallest eigenvalues respectively. We assume
\lambda_{0}>0
.

The TD learning algorithm

Stochastic approximation (SA) is a standard tool to solve the fixed point equation problems of the form (
15
) given a sequence of random observations
\{(\bm{A}_{t},\bm{b}_{t})\}_{t\geq 1}.

When specialized to the above setting, it is referred to as the Temporal Difference (TD) learning algorithm
(
Sutton, 1988
)
.
Specifically, given a sequence of pre-selected step size
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq 1}
and an initial estimator
\bm{\theta}_{0}=\bm{0}
, TD proceeds using the updating rule

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\theta}_{t-1}-\eta_{t}(\bm{A}_{t}\bm{\theta}_{t-1}-\bm{b}_{t}),

(18)

where

\displaystyle\bm{A}_{t}:=\bm{\phi}(s_{t-1})\left(\bm{\phi}(s_{t-1})-\gamma\bm{\phi}(s_{t})\right)^{\top}\quad\text{and }\quad\bm{b}_{t}:=\bm{\phi}(s_{t-1})r_{t-1}.

(19)

After
T
iterations, we deploy Polyak-Ruppert averaging
(
Polyak and Juditsky, 1992
;
Ruppert, 1998
)
and compute

\displaystyle\overline{\bm{\theta}}_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{\theta}_{t},

(20)

as the estimator for
\bm{\theta}^{\star}
. In this work, we follow the precedent of
Polyak and Juditsky (1992)
;
Ruppert (1998)
and choose
polynomial-decaying
stepsizes
\eta_{t}=\eta_{0}t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
.

4.2
Convergence and Berry-Essen bounds for TD estimator

It is known from the literature on stochastic approximations that, in fixed dimensions, the TD estimator with polynomial-decaying stepsizes and Polyak-Ruppert averaging
\overline{\bm{\theta}}_{T}
satisfies the central limit theorem (CLT)
(
Fort, 2015
;
Mou et al., 2020
;
Li et al., 2023
, see, e.g.,)

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}(\overline{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-\bm{\theta}^{\star})\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}),

with asymptotic covariance matrix
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}=\bm{A}^{-1}\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}\bm{A}^{-\top},

(21)

where
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
is the time-averaging covariance matrix

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathbf{Var}_{s_{0}\sim\mu,s_{t+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{t})}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\bm{A}_{t}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}]+\sum_{t=2}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})(\bm{A}_{t}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{t})^{\top}+(\bm{A}_{t}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{t})(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}].

(22)

However, the above results are asymptotic in nature and do not explicitly reveal the dependence on the dimension and other problem-related quantities. A line of recent work has made headway toward this goal, attaining non-asymptotic distributional characterization of the TD procedure.
Nonetheless, most of the literature has focused on the independent setting where each
(\bm{A}_{t},\bm{b}_{t})
pair is an independent and identically distributed random variable
(
Mou et al., 2020
;
Wu et al., 2024
;
Samsonov et al., 2024a
, see, e.g.,)
.
To describe our results, throughout this section, we make the additional assumption that the Markov chain mixes exponentially fast, a standard condition in finite sample analyses of Markovian data.

Assumption 4.1
.

There exists constants
m>0,\rho\in(0,1)
, such that for every positive integer
t
,

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P^{t}(\cdot\mid s),\mu)\leq m\rho^{t}.

We remark that, under this assumption, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the corresponding
mixing time

\displaystyle t_{\mathsf{mix}}(\varepsilon):=\min\{t:\sup_{s\in\mathcal{S}}d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P^{t}(\cdot\mid s),\mu)\leq\varepsilon\},

(23)

satisfies the bound

\displaystyle t_{\mathsf{mix}}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{\log(m/\varepsilon)}{\log(1/\rho)}.

(24)

Theorem 4.2

(High-probability convergence of TD estimator)
.

Consider TD with Polyak-Ruppert averaging (
4.1
) with Markov samples and decaying stepsizes
\eta_{t}=\eta_{0}t^{-\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. Suppose that the Markov transition kernel has a unique stationary distribution, has a positive spectral gap, mixes exponentially as indicated by Assumption
4.1
, and starts from a distribution satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Then for every tolerance level
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists
\eta_{0}=\eta_{0}(\delta)
such that

\displaystyle\|\overline{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{Tr}(\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star})}{T}\log\frac{1}{\delta}}+o\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}}\log^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{d}{\delta}\right),

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Theorem
4.2
, proved in Appendix
C.3
, establishes the first high-probability convergence guarantee for the TD estimation error with Markov samples that matches the asymptotic variance
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}/T
up to a log factor. It may be regarded as the Markovian counterpart of Theorem 3.1 in
Wu et al. (2024)
, which focuses on the TD estimation error with
independent
samples under the same settings.

Our analysis of the TD estimation error borrows tools and ideas from several previous contributions, starting from the seminal work of
Polyak and Juditsky (1992)
and including some of the most recent results; see, e.g,
Li et al. (2023)
;
Samsonov et al. (2024a)
;
Srikant (2025)
. In particular, we apply the induction technique of
Srikant and Ying (2019)
,
Li et al. (2024)
and
Wu et al. (2024)
, developed for TD learning with
independent
samples. The generalization from independent to Markov samples is highly nontrivial, and the novel theoretical results obtained in the first part of the paper, especially the newly-established matrix Bernstein inequality for Markovian martingales (Corollary
3.1
), play a critical role in our analysis.

Dependence on problem-related quantities

We point out that the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}
depends on the probability tolerance level
\delta
as well as other problem-related quantities, as specified in Eq. (
107
) in Appendix
C.2
. Though not ideal, this dependence also appears in the independent setting in
(
Wu et al., 2024
, Theorem 3.1)
, who further argues that it is unavoidable. It is also notable that the choice of
\eta_{0}
does
not
depend on the sample size
T
, as is the case in
Samsonov et al. (2024b)
, who also considers TD with Markov samples, though with a
time-invariant
stepsize choice.
The upper bound in Theorem
4.2
depends on various problem-related quantities, including the mixing speed of the Markov chain (specifically, the mixing factor
\rho
and the spectral gap
1-\lambda
), the discount factor
\gamma
, the initial stepsize
\eta_{0}
, the feature covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}
and the time-averaging variance matrix of the TD error,
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
. These intricate dependencies impact the higher-order (in
T
) reminder terms, which is not shown in our bound but can be tracked in our proofs.

In our next and final result, we give a novel high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for the TD estimator assuming Markovian data.

Theorem 4.3

(Berry–Esseen bound for TD estimator)
.

Consider TD with Polyak-Ruppert averaging (
4.1
) with Markov samples and decaying stepsizes
\eta_{t}=\eta_{0}t^{-\frac{3}{4}}
, where
\eta_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
. Suppose that the Markov transition kernel has a unique stationary distribution, has a positive spectral gap, mixes exponentially as indicated by Assumption
4.1
, and is initiated from a distribution
\nu
satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Further assume that
\lambda_{\min}(\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}})>0
. Then when
T
is sufficiently large
2
2

2

The exact constraint on
T
is indicated in (
48
) in the Appendix.
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{C}}(\sqrt{T}(\overline{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-\bm{\theta}^{\star}),\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}))\leq\widetilde{C}T^{-\frac{1}{4}}\log T+o(T^{-\frac{1}{4}}),

where
\widetilde{C}
is a problem-related quantity independent of
T
, with exact form shown in Appendix
C.4
.

In a nutshell, Theorem
4.3
, whose proof is given in Appendix
C.4
, ensures that the rescaled TD estimator with Polyak-Ruppert averaging converges to its Gaussian limit at a rate of
T^{-1/4}\log T
(holding all other parameters fixed) with respect to the convex distance.
The dependence on the feature dimension
d
, as well as other problem-related parameters, is unwieldy and thus not given explicitly in the statement of the theorem. However, in principle it can be tracked through the various steps of the proof.

The closest contribution to ours, and indeed the impetus for some of our work, is the recent excellent paper by
Srikant (2025)
, which claims the bound

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{W}}(\sqrt{T}(\overline{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-\bm{\theta}^{\star}),\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}))

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{C}T^{-\frac{1}{6}}\log T.

where
\widetilde{C}
is a problem-related quantity independent of
T
. In our analysis, we have filled in the gaps in some of their arguments concerning vector-valued martingales and their application to TD learning, while also strengthening the bound from Wasserstein to convex distance.
Other recent and directly relevant contributions by
Samsonov et al. (2024a)
and
Wu et al. (2024)
have also produced high-dimensional Berry–Esseen bound for TD learning with
independent samples
, with
Wu et al. (2024)
claiming the the state-of-the-art rate (in
T
) of
O(T^{-\frac{1}{3}})
. Though it remains to be seen whether the rate of convergence of order
O(T^{-\frac{1}{4}}\log T)
for Markovian data that we obtain in Theorem
4.3
is sharp, the generalization from independent to Markov samples is nonetheless highly nontrivial and, we believe, significant. In any case, we are able to show in Appendix
C.5
that, for any
\alpha>\frac{3}{4}
and when
T
is sufficiently large,

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{C}}(\sqrt{T}(\overline{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-\bm{\theta}^{\star}),\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}))

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{C}T^{-\frac{1}{4}}\log T,\quad\text{for all }\alpha\in\left(\frac{3}{4},1\right).

(25)

Here
\widetilde{C}
is a problem-related quantity independent of
T
. This lower bound provides a partial clue that our rate might in fact be optimal.

5
Discussion and future directions

In this paper, we derive novel, high-dimensional uncertainty quantification results for Markov chain induced martingales, including a Bernstein-style concentration inequality and a Berry–Esseen bound. Through the process, we construct improved Berry–Esseen bounds for martingales with fixed terminal quadratic variance, which may be of independent interest.

We apply these results to obtain state-of-the-art finite sample convergence guarantees and Gaussian approximations for TD learning with Markovian observation.
While this work addresses a wide range of theoretical problems, it also points to several directions for future research. First, our Berry–Esseen bound for the convex distance in Theorem
3.2
exhibits a polynomial dependence on the dimension
d
. This is a feature of virtually all such bounds from the literature, most of which are concerned with independent sums; see, e.g.,
Bentkus (2003)
;
Bentkus (2005)
;
Schulte and Yukich (2019)
;
Fang and Koike (2024)
;
Raič (2019)
;
Nourdin et al. (2022)
;
Cattaneo et al. (2022)
.
A poly-logarithmic dependence in
d
holds for the sub-class of hyper-rectangles; see
Kojevnikov and Song (2022b)
. In regards to other applications, beyond TD learning, Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
provide novel tools for analyzing the statistical properties of other Markov chain-based ML algorithms like MCMC, general stochastic approximation and Q-learning.
However, we remark that, in order to carry out statistical inference, it is necessary to have an (ideally efficient) estimator of the variance matrix
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}
. The construction and convergence rate of any such estimator remain important open problems in the field.
Finally, whether it is possible to close the gap between the
O(T^{-\frac{1}{4}}\log T)
rate in the Berry–Esseen bound for TD with Markov data in Theorem
4.3
and the
O(T^{-\frac{1}{3}})
rate for its independent-sample counterpart
Wu et al. (2024)
is also an interesting problem.

Ackowledgement

W. Wu and A.Rinaldo were partially supported in part by NIH under Grant R01 NS121913 and NSF award DMS-2113611.
Y. Wei is supported in part by the NSF grants CCF-2106778, CCF-2418156 and CAREER award DMS-2143215. We thank Bobby Shi for making us aware of Theorem 2.5 of
Neeman et al. (2024)
, giving the same inequality as in Theorem
3.5
.

Appendix A
Preliminary facts

A.1
Markov chain basics

A.1.1
Stationary distribution and corresponding norm

Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the state space of the homogeneous, discrete time Markov chain, which we assumed throughout to be a Borel space endowed with it Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{F}
. For every
x\in\mathcal{S}
, the
transition kernel

P(x,\cdot)
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{S}
defined as

\displaystyle P(x,B)=\mathbb{P}(s_{2}\in B\mid s_{1}=x),\quad B\in\mathscr{F}

which is guaranteed to be a regular conditional distribution.
For any integer
k>0
, the
k
th power of the kernel
P
is the regular conditional probability distribution defined iteratively as

P^{k}(x,B)=\int_{\mathcal{S}}P(x,dy)P^{k-1}(y,B)=\mathbb{P}(s_{k}\in B|s_{0}=x),\quad x\in\mathcal{S},B\in\mathscr{F},

where we define
P^{0}(x,B)=\mathsf{1}_{\{x\in B\}}
.
We will assume throughout that the Markov chain admits a (unique!)
stationary distribution

\mu
, a probability distribution on
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{F})
satisfying

\displaystyle\mu(B)=\int_{\mathcal{S}}P(y,B)\mu(\mathrm{d}y),\quad\forall B\in\mathscr{F}.

For a vector-valued function
\bm{g}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
\mu(\bm{g})
to denote
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu}[\bm{g}(x)]
, if well-defined. Furthermore, for simplicity, we use
\bm{g}^{\parallel}
and
\bm{g}^{\perp}
to denote the functions

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{\parallel}(x)=\mu(\bm{g}),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\bm{g}^{\perp}(x)=\bm{g}(x)-\bm{g}^{\parallel}(x),\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{S},

provided that
\mu(\bm{g})
exists.
We denote with
L_{2}(\mu)
the space of Borel functions
\bm{f}\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\int_{\mathcal{S}}\|\bm{f}(x)\|^{2}\mu(dx)<\infty
. Then,
L_{2}(\mu)
is a Hilbert space, with
inner product
given by

\displaystyle\langle\bm{g}_{1},\bm{g}_{2}\rangle_{\mu}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\bm{g}_{1}^{\top}(x)\bm{g}_{2}(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x),\quad\bm{g}_{1},\bm{g}_{2}\in L_{2}(\mu),

which, in turn, induces the
\mu
-norm

\displaystyle\|\bm{g}\|_{\mu}=\left(\int\|\bm{g}(x)\|_{2}^{2}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\bm{g}\in L_{2}(\mu).

Note that, for a fixed vector
\bm{v}
,
\|\bm{v}\|_{\mu}
reduces to the Euclidean norm
\|\bm{v}\|_{2}
.
We shall interchange these two ways of writing for ease of exposition.

To the transition kernel
P
admitting a stationary distribution
\mu
there corresponds a bounded linear operator, denoted as
\mathcal{P}
, which maps a vector-valued function
\bm{g}\in L_{2}(\mu)
to another vector-valued function in
L_{2}(\mu)
, denoted as
\mathcal{P}\bm{g}
, given by

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{P}\bm{g}(x)=\int_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\bm{g}(y)P(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{2}\sim P(\cdot\mid x)}[\bm{g}(s_{2})\mid s_{1}=x],

where, with a slight abuse of notation, we write
P(\cdot\mid s_{1})
for
P(s_{1},\cdot)
. Accordingly, for any positive integer
k
, we define the operator
\mathcal{P}^{k}
over
L_{2}(\mu)
by

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{g}(x)=\int_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\bm{g}(y)P^{k}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\mathbb{E}_{s_{k}\sim P^{k}(\cdot\mid x)}[\bm{g}(s_{k})\mid s_{0}=x].

The
adjoint operator
of
\mathcal{P}
, denoted as
\mathcal{P}^{*}
, is a bounded operator mapping a vector-valued function
\bm{g}\in L_{2}(\mu)
to another vector-valued function in
L_{2}(\mu)
, denoted as
\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}
such that

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{P}\bm{g}_{1},\bm{g}_{2}\rangle_{\mu}=\langle\bm{g}_{1},\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}_{2}\rangle_{\mu}.

(26)

The
adjoint transition kernel

P^{*}
is the transition kernel (regular conditional probability) satisfying

\displaystyle\int_{A}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)P^{*}(x,B)=\int_{B}P(y,A)\mu(\mathrm{d}y),\quad\forall A,B\in\mathscr{F}.

Accordingly,
\mathcal{P}^{*}
maps any
\bm{g}\in L_{2}(\mu)
to a new function
\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}\in L_{2}(\mu)
given by

\displaystyle x\in\mathcal{S}\mapsto\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}(x)=\int_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\bm{g}(y)P^{*}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\mathbb{E}[\bm{g}(s_{1})\mid s_{2}=x],

and condition (
26
) can be equivalently expressed as

\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mu(dx)P^{*}(x,dy)\bm{f}^{\top}(x)\bm{g}(y)=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mu(dy)P(y,dx)\bm{f}^{\top}(x)\bm{g}(y),\quad\forall\bm{f},\bm{g}\in L_{2}(\mu).

A Markov chain is called
self-adjoint
, or
reversible
, if
P=P^{*}
or, equivalently, if
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{*}
.

Finally, with a minor abuse of notation, for every measure
\nu
on
(\mathcal{S},\mathscr{F})
, we use
\mathcal{P}\nu
to denote the measure defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\nu(B)=\int_{\mathcal{S}}P(y,B)\nu(\mathrm{d}y),\quad\forall B\in\mathscr{F}.

By definition, the stationary distribution
\mu
satisfies
\mathcal{P}\mu=\mu
.

A.1.2
Spectral gap

For a Markov chain with transition kernel
P
and unique stationary distribution
\mu
, let
\mathcal{L}_{2}
denote the Hilbert space

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{2}:=\{g:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}\mid\|g\|_{\mu}<\infty\}.

The
spectral gap
of the Markov chain is defined as
1-\lambda
, where

\displaystyle\lambda=\lambda(P):=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{L}_{2},\mu(g)=0}\frac{\|\mathcal{P}g\|_{\mu}}{\|g\|_{\mu}}.

We now present some properties of the spectral gap.

Proposition A.1
.

For any Markov chain with transition kernel
P
, as long as
\lambda(P)
and the adjoint transition kernel
P^{*}
are both well-defined, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\lambda(P)=\lambda(P^{*}).

Proof.

Define
\Pi
as the projection of any function onto the subspace of constant functions:

\displaystyle\Pi g(x):=\mu(g),\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{S}.

It is easy to verify that
\lambda(P)
is equal to the operator norm of
\mathcal{P}-\Pi
, and that
\lambda(P^{*})
is equal to the operator norm of
\mathcal{P}^{*}-\Pi
. Furthermore, the operators
\mathcal{P}-\Pi
and
\mathcal{P}^{*}-\Pi
are adjoint. Therefore, the proposition follows from the facts that adjoint operators have the same spectrum in Hilbert spaces.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

For any dimension
d
, and
\bm{g}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it can be guaranteed that as long as
\mu(\bm{g})=\bm{0}
and
\|\bm{g}\|_{\mu}<\infty
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}\|_{\mu}\leq\lambda\|\bm{g}\|_{\mu}

Proof.

This property is easy to verify by representing
\bm{g}=\{g_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}
.
∎

Proposition A.3
.

For any
\bm{g}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if
\mu(\bm{g})=\bm{0}
, then
\mu(\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g})=0
and therefore
\|\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}\|_{\mu}\leq\lambda\|\bm{g}\|_{\mu}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mu(\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g})
is featured by

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g})

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathcal{S}}\left[\int_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\bm{g}(y)\frac{P(y,\mathrm{d}x)}{\mu(\mathrm{d}x)}\mu(\mathrm{d}y)\right]\mu(\mathrm{d}x)

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\bm{g}(y)\left[\int_{x\in\mathcal{S}}P(y,\mathrm{d}x)\right]\mu(\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in\mathcal{S}}\bm{g}(y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)=\mu(\bm{g})=\bm{0}.

Consequently,
\|\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g}\|_{\mu}\leq\lambda\|\bm{g}\|_{\mu}
follows from Proposition
A.2
.
∎

As a direct corollary of Proposition
A.3
, it is easy to verify that
(\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g})^{\parallel}=\bm{g}^{\parallel}
and
(\mathcal{P}^{*}\bm{g})^{\perp}=\mathcal{P}^{*}(\bm{g}^{\perp})
.

A.2
Algebraic facts

Supplementary notation

For any complex number
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we use
\mathsf{Re}(z)
to denote its real part. If a matrix
\bm{M}
is positive (semi-)definite, i.e.
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{M})>(\geq)0
, we write
\bm{M}\succ(\succeq)\bm{0}
; when two matrices
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
satisfy
\bm{X}-\bm{Y}\succ(\succeq)\bm{0}
, we write
\bm{X}\succ(\succeq)\bm{Y}
or equivalently
\bm{Y}\prec(\preceq)\bm{X}
.
For a vector
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
\{x_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}
to denote its entries; for a matrix
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, we use
\{X_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n}
to denote its entries. The
vectorization
of matrix
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, denoted as
\textbf{vec}(\bm{X})
, is defined as a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{mn}
with entries
[\textbf{vec}(\bm{X})]_{(i-1)m+j}=\bm{X}_{ij}
for every
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
.
For any matrices
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, and
\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, their
Kronecker product
, denoted as
\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y}
, is a matrix of the size of
mp\times nq
, and can be written in blocked form as

\displaystyle\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y}=\begin{bmatrix}X_{11}\bm{Y}&X_{12}\bm{Y}&\ldots&X_{1n}\bm{Y}\\
X_{21}\bm{Y}&X_{22}\bm{Y}&\ldots&X_{2n}\bm{Y}\\
\ldots&\ldots&\ldots&\ldots\\
X_{m1}\bm{Y}&X_{m2}\bm{Y}&\ldots&X_{mn}\bm{Y}\end{bmatrix}.

With subscripts, it can be represented as
(\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y})_{(i-1)m+j,(k-1)p+\ell}=X_{ik}Y_{j\ell}
.

A.2.1
General algebraic theorems

Theorem A.4

(Properties of the Kronecker product)
.

The following properties hold:

(1)

Let
\bm{X},\bm{Y},\bm{Z},\bm{W}
be four matrices such that the matrix products
\bm{XZ}
and
\bm{YW}
are well-defined. Then
(\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y})(\bm{Z}\otimes\bm{W})=(\bm{XZ})\otimes\bm{YW}
;

(2)

As a direct consequence of (1), let
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
, then
\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y}=(\bm{X}\otimes\bm{I}_{p})(\bm{I}_{n}\otimes\bm{Y})
;

(3)

For any matrices
\bm{X},\bm{Y}
,
(\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y})^{\top}=\bm{X}^{\top}\otimes\bm{Y}^{\top}
;

(4)

For any matrices
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
and
\bm{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times n}
, it can be guaranteed that
(\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B})\textbf{vec}(\bm{Z})=\textbf{vec}(\bm{Y}\bm{Z}\bm{X}^{\top})
;

(5)

As a direct consequence of (4), let
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then
\bm{Xy}=(\bm{I}_{m}\otimes\bm{y})\textbf{vec}(\bm{X})
;

(6)

Also as a direct consequence of (5), let
\bm{X},\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, then
\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{XY}^{\top})=[\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})]^{\top}(\bm{X}\otimes\bm{Y})\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})
;

(7)

For any
\bm{X},\bm{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, it can be guaranteed that
\exp(\bm{X})\otimes\exp(\bm{Y})=\exp(\bm{X}\otimes\bm{I}_{d^{2}}+\bm{I}_{d^{2}}\otimes\bm{Y})
.

Theorem A.5
.

For any positive definite functions
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\mathbb{S}^{d\times d}
, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{A}^{-1}(\bm{A}-\bm{B}))\leq\mathsf{Tr}(\log(\bm{A})-\log(\bm{B})).

Proof.

This conclusion is a direct application of the Klein’s inequality (see, for example, Theorem 2.11 of
Carlen [2009]
), which states that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(f(\bm{A})-f(\bm{B})-(\bm{A}-\bm{B})^{\top}f^{\prime}(\bm{A}))\geq 0

(27)

for any concave function
f:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, on the concave function
f(x)=\log x
. For the specific definition of
f(\bm{X})
where
\bm{X}
is a positive semidefinite matrix, and the proof of the inequality (
27
), we refer the readers to
Carlen [2009]
.
∎

A.2.2
Properties of the matrices involved in the paper regarding TD learning

In this section, we present several useful algebraic results, most of which were established by
Wu et al. [2024]
.
The following lemma reveals several critical algebraic features of the matrix
\bm{A}
.

Lemma A.6

(Lemma A.4 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

Let
\bm{A}_{t},\bm{A}
be defined as in (
19
) and (
16
) respectively, and
\bm{\Sigma}
be defined as in (
17
) with
\lambda_{0}
and
\lambda_{\Sigma}
being its smallest and largest eigenvalues. Then the following features hold:

\displaystyle 2(1-\gamma)\bm{\Sigma}\preceq\bm{A}+\bm{A}^{\top}\preceq 2(1+\gamma)\bm{\Sigma};

(28)

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq i\leq d}\mathsf{Re}(\lambda_{i}(\bm{A}))\geq(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0};

(29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{A}_{t}^{\top}\bm{A}_{t}]\preceq\bm{A}+\bm{A}^{\top};

(30)

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{\top}\bm{A}\preceq\lambda_{\Sigma}(\bm{A}+\bm{A}^{\top});

(31)

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{I}-\eta\bm{A}\right\|\leq 1-\frac{1-\gamma}{2}\lambda_{0}\eta,\quad\forall\eta\in\left(0,\frac{1}{2\lambda_{\Sigma}}\right);

(32)

\displaystyle\|\bm{A}^{-1}\|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(1-\gamma)}.

(33)

Notice that the bound (
33
) directly implies that

\displaystyle\|\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}\leq\|\bm{A}^{-1}\|\|\bm{b}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(1-\gamma)}.

(34)

Lemma A.7

(Lemma A.5 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

For any matrix
\bm{E}\succeq\bm{0}
, there exists a unique positive definite matrix
\bm{X}\succeq\bm{0}
, such that

\displaystyle\bm{AX}+\bm{XA}^{\top}=\bm{E}.

Furthermore, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\|\bm{X}\|\leq\frac{1}{2(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}}\|\bm{E}\|,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{X})\leq\frac{1}{2(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}}\mathsf{Tr}(\bm{E}).

The following lemma further features the compressive power of the matrix
\prod_{k=1}^{t}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A})
.

Lemma A.8

(Lemma A.6 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

Assume that
\beta\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
. Let
\eta_{t}=\eta_{0}t^{-\alpha}
for all positive integer
t
, then the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle t^{\alpha}\prod_{k=1}^{t}\left(1-\beta k^{-\alpha}\right)\leq e\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}};

(35)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{t}i^{-\nu}\prod_{k=i+1}^{t}\left(1-\beta k^{-\alpha}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\nu-1}\left(\frac{2(\nu-\alpha)}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{\nu-\alpha}{1-\alpha}}t^{\alpha-\nu},\quad\forall\nu\in(1,\alpha+1];

(36)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{t}i^{-2\alpha}\prod_{k=i+1}^{t}\left(1-\beta k^{-\alpha}\right)=\frac{1}{\beta}t^{-\alpha}+o\left(t^{-\alpha}\right);

(37)

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq t}i^{-\alpha}\prod_{k=i+1}^{t}\left(1-\beta k^{-\alpha}\right)\leq e\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}t^{-\alpha}.

(38)

Lemma A.9

(Lemma A.7 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

For every
\beta\in(0,1)
,
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and sufficiently large
T
, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle t^{-\alpha}\sum_{j=t}^{T}\prod_{k=t+1}^{j}(1-\beta k^{-\alpha})<3\left(\frac{2}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad\text{and}

(39)

\displaystyle t^{-\alpha}\sum_{j=t}^{\infty}\prod_{k=t+1}^{j}(1-\beta k^{-\alpha})-\frac{1}{\beta}\lesssim\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)t^{\alpha-1}.

(40)

Lemma A.10

(Lemma A.8 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

Let
\eta_{t}=\eta_{0}t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
0\leq\eta_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2\lambda_{\Sigma}}
. For every
t\in[T]
, define
\bm{Q}_{t}
as

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{t}=\eta_{t}\sum_{j=t}^{T}\prod_{k=t+1}^{j}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A})

(41)

Then for any positive integer
\ell<T
, the following relation hold:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}(\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{A}^{-1})=-\bm{A}^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\prod_{k=1}^{j}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A}),

(42a)

Furthermore, there exist a sequence of matrix
\{\bm{S}_{t}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
, such that the difference between
\bm{Q}_{t}
and
\bm{A}^{-1}
can be represented as

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{A}^{-1}=\bm{S}_{t}-\bm{A}^{-1}\prod_{k=t}^{T}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A}),

(42b)

in which
\bm{S}_{t}
is independent of
T
, and its norm is bounded by

\displaystyle\|\bm{S}_{t}\|\lesssim\eta_{0}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\left(\frac{2}{(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}\eta_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}t^{\alpha-1}.

(42c)

Lemma A.11

(Lemma A.10 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

Let
\eta_{t}=\eta_{0}t^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and
0\leq\eta\leq\frac{1}{2\lambda_{\Sigma}}
. For
\bm{Q}_{t}
defined as in (
41
), it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\|\bm{Q}_{t}\|\leq 3\eta_{0}^{-\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}\left(\frac{4\alpha}{\lambda_{0}(1-\gamma)}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\quad\forall t\in[T];

(43)

\displaystyle\|\bm{A}\bm{Q}_{t}\|\leq 2+\eta_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)t^{\alpha-1};\quad\text{and}

(44)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{A}^{-1}\|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(1-\gamma)}T^{\alpha-1}+\widetilde{C}T^{2\alpha-2}

(45)

where
\beta=\frac{1-\gamma}{2}\lambda_{0}\eta_{0}
and
\widetilde{C}
depends on
\alpha,\eta_{0},\lambda_{0},\gamma
.

The following lemma bounds the norms and trace of the time-averaging variance matrix
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
.

Lemma A.12
.

Under Assumption
4.1
, when
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
is defined as in (
22
), it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}\|\leq\|\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}\leq\mathsf{Tr}(\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}})\leq\left(1+\frac{2m}{1-\rho}\right)(2\|\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}+1)^{2}\lesssim\frac{m}{1-\rho}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}(1-\gamma)}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

The first two inequalities follow directly from the fact that
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
, a variance-covariance matrix, is positive semi-definite; the last inequality follows directly from (
34
). It now boils down to proving that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}})\leq\left(1+\frac{2m}{1-\rho}\right)(2\|\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}+1).

For every integer
\ell>1
, we firstly use the iteration of expectations to derive

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(\mathbb{E}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})^{\top}])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim\mu,s_{1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{0}),s_{\ell}\sim P^{\ell-1}(\cdot\mid s_{1}),s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim\mu,s_{1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{0})}[\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell}\sim P^{\ell-1}(\cdot\mid s_{1})}[\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})\mid\mathscr{F}_{1}]];

meanwhile, by definition,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell}\sim\mu}[\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})\mid\mathscr{F}_{1}]

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell}\sim\mu,s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}[\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1}]=0.

Therefore, the basic property of TV distance and Assumption
4.1
guarantees

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell}\sim P^{\ell-1}(\cdot\mid s_{1})}[\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})\mid\mathscr{F}_{1}]

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell}\sim P^{\ell-1}(\cdot\mid s_{1})}-\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell}\sim\mu}\right)[\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})\mid\mathscr{F}_{1}]]

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P^{\ell-1}(\cdot\mid s_{1}),\mu)\cdot\sup_{s_{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})\mid\mathscr{F}_{1}]

\displaystyle\leq m\rho^{\ell-1}\cdot(2\|\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}+1)^{2}.

By taking expectations with regard to
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(\mathbb{E}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})^{\top}])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{s_{0}\sim\mu,s_{1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{0})}[\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell}\sim P^{\ell-1}(\cdot\mid s_{1})}[\mathbb{E}_{s_{\ell+1}\sim P(\cdot\mid s_{\ell})}(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})^{\top}(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})\mid\mathscr{F}_{1}]]

\displaystyle\leq m\rho^{\ell-1}\cdot(2\|\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}+1)^{2}.

Hence by definition, the trace of
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\|\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{Tr}(\mathbb{E}[(\bm{A}_{1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{1})(\bm{A}_{\ell+1}\bm{\theta}^{\star}-\bm{b}_{\ell+1})^{\top}])

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}m\rho^{\ell-1})(2\|\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}+1)^{2}=\left(1+\frac{2m}{1-\rho}\right)(2\|\bm{\theta}^{\star}\|_{2}+1)^{2}.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Theorem A.13

(Analogy to Theorem A.11 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

Define
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{Q}_{t}\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top},

(46)

and let
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}
be defined as in (
21
). Define
\bm{X}(\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star})
as the unique solution to the Lyapunov equation

\displaystyle\eta_{0}(\bm{AX+XA}^{\top})=\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star};

(47)

then as
T\to\infty
, the difference between
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}
and
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}
is featured by

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}-\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}-T^{\alpha-1}\bm{X}(\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star})\|\leq\widetilde{C}T^{2\alpha-2}\|\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}\|,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}-\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}-T^{\alpha-1}\bm{X}(\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}))\leq\widetilde{C}T^{2\alpha-2}\mathsf{Tr}(\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}).

Here,
\widetilde{C}
is a problem-related quantity that can be represented by
\eta_{0},\alpha
and
\gamma
.

Proof.

This theorem can be proved by replacing
\overline{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}
by
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}
,
\bm{\Lambda}^{\star}
by
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}
and
\bm{\Gamma}
by
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
in the proof of Theorem A.11 in
Wu et al. [2024]
. Details are omitted.
∎

Lemma A.14

(Analogy to Lemma A.12 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

Let
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}
and
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}
be defined as in (
46
) and (
21
) respectively. When

\displaystyle T\geq 4\left(\frac{2}{(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}\eta_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}(1-\alpha)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}\Gamma(\frac{1}{1-\alpha})\mathsf{cond}(\Gamma),

(48)

it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(\bm{A}\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}\bm{A}^{\top})\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\min}(\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}})=\frac{1}{2\|\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}^{-1}\|}.

Proof.

This lemma can be proved by replacing
\overline{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}
by
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}_{T}
,
\bm{\Lambda}^{\star}
by
\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\star}
and
\bm{\Gamma}
by
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}
in the proof of Lemma A.12 in
Wu et al. [2024]
. Details are omitted.
∎

Lemma A.15
.

Let
\bm{Q}_{t}
be defined as in (
41
), then it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t-1}\|\lesssim\eta_{0}\left[\eta_{0}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)+\alpha\right]\left(\frac{2}{(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}\eta_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}\frac{\log T}{T}.

Proof.

By definition, the matrices
\bm{Q}_{t-1}
and
\bm{Q}_{t}
can be related by

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{t-1}\bm{I}+\eta_{t-1}\sum_{j=t}^{T}\prod_{k=t}^{j}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A})

\displaystyle=\eta_{t-1}\bm{I}+\frac{\eta_{t-1}}{\eta_{t}}(\bm{I}-\eta_{t}\bm{A})\bm{Q}_{t};

Therefore, the difference between
\bm{Q}_{t}
and
\bm{Q}_{t-1}
is featured by

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t-1}

\displaystyle=-\eta_{t-1}(\bm{I}-\bm{AQ}_{t})-\left(\frac{\eta_{t-1}}{\eta_{t}}-1\right)\bm{Q}_{t}

\displaystyle=\eta_{t-1}\bm{AS}_{t}-\eta_{t-1}\prod_{k=t}^{T}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A})-\left(\frac{\eta_{t-1}}{\eta_{t}}-1\right)\bm{Q}_{t}

where we invoked (
42b
) in the last equation. Hence by triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\|\bm{A}\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t-1}\|\bm{S}_{t}\|+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t-1}\left\|\prod_{k=t}^{T}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A})\right\|+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\frac{\eta_{t-1}}{\eta_{t}}-1\right)\|\bm{Q}_{t}\|.

By (
42c
), (
43
) and the telescoping method, it is easy to verify that the three terms on the right-hand-side can be bounded respectively by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\|\bm{A}\|\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t-1}\|\bm{S}_{t}\|\lesssim\eta_{0}^{2}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\right)\left(\frac{2}{(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}\eta_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}\frac{\log T}{T},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\eta_{t-1}\left\|\prod_{k=t}^{T}(\bm{I}-\eta_{k}\bm{A})\right\|\leq\frac{2}{(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}}\frac{1}{T},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\frac{\eta_{t-1}}{\eta_{t}}-1\right)\|\bm{Q}_{t}\|\lesssim\alpha\left(\frac{2}{(1-\gamma)\lambda_{0}\eta_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}\frac{\log T}{T}.

The lemma follows immediately.
∎

A.3
Other basic facts

The following theorem proposed by
Devroye et al. [2018]
gives an upper bound for the total variation (TV) distance between two Gaussian random variables with same means and different covariance matrices.

Theorem A.16

(
Devroye et al. [2018]
)
.

Let
\bm{\Lambda}_{1},\bm{\Lambda}_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{d\times d}
be two positive definite matrices, and
\bm{\mu}
be any vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then the TV distance between Gaussian distributions
\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Lambda}_{1})
and
\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Lambda}_{2})
is bounded by

\displaystyle\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{100}\left\|\bm{\Lambda}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{\Lambda}_{2}\bm{\Lambda}_{1}^{-1/2}-\bm{I}_{d}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}\right\}\leq d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Lambda}_{1}),\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Lambda}_{2}))\leq\frac{3}{2}\left\|\bm{\Lambda}_{1}^{-1/2}\bm{\Lambda}_{2}\bm{\Lambda}_{1}^{-1/2}-\bm{I}_{d}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}.

Theorem A.17

(
Nazarov [2003]
)
.

Let
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Lambda})
be a
d
-dimensional Gaussian random variable, and
\mathcal{A}
be any non-convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any
\varepsilon\geq 0
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon}:=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\inf_{y\in\mathcal{A}}\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{-\varepsilon}:=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:B(\bm{x},\varepsilon)\subset\mathcal{A}\},

(49)

where
B(\bm{x},\varepsilon)
represents the
d
-dimensional ball centered at
\bm{x}
with radius
\varepsilon
. Then it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{z}\in\mathcal{A}^{\varepsilon}-\mathcal{A})\lesssim\|\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon,\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{z}\in\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{A}^{-\varepsilon})\lesssim\|\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon.

The following theorem from
Nourdin et al. [2022]
relates the distance on convex sets with the Wasserstein distance when one of the distributions being compared corresponds to a Gaussian random variable.

Theorem A.18
.

Let
\bm{x}
be a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\bm{y}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Lambda})
where
\bm{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{S}^{d\times d}
is positive-definite. Then it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{C}}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\lesssim\|\bm{\Lambda}^{-1}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{d_{\mathsf{W}}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}.

We also recall the following concentration inequalities.

Theorem A.19

(Corollary A.16 of
Wu et al. [2024]
)
.

Let
\{\bm{x}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
be a martingale in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
W_{\max}
be a positive constant that satisfies

\displaystyle W_{\max}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{t}\|\bm{x}_{i}-\bm{x}_{i-1}\|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{almost surely.}

Then it can be guaranteed with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\|\bm{x}_{t}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{2W_{\max}\log\frac{3}{\delta}}.

Theorem A.20

(Theorem 2.1 of
Peng et al. [2024]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a Hilbert space,
\{\bm{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be an
\mathcal{X}
-valued martingale difference sequence adapted to the filtration
\{\mathscr{F}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
,
\bm{Y}_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\bm{X}_{j}
be the corresponding martingale, and
W_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\mathbb{E}\|\bm{X}_{j}\|^{2}\mid\mathscr{F}_{j-1}
. Suppose that
\max_{i\in[n]}\|\bm{X}_{i}\|\leq b
for some constant
b>0
. Then for any
\varepsilon
and
\sigma>0
, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\exists k\in[n],\text{ s.t. }\|\bm{Y}_{k}\|\geq\varepsilon\text{ and }W_{k}\leq\sigma^{2}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}/2}{\sigma^{2}+b\varepsilon/3}\right).

As a direct consequence, under the conditions of Theorem
A.20
, for every
\delta\in(0,1)
it can be guaranteed with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle W_{n}>\sigma^{2}\quad\text{or}\quad\|\bm{Y}_{n}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\log\frac{1}{\delta}}+\frac{b}{n}\log\frac{1}{\delta}.

Theorem A.21

(Theorem 1 of
Fan et al. [2021]
)
.

Let
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a Markov chain on state space
\mathcal{S}
with a unique stationary distribution
\mu
and a positive spectral gap
1-\lambda>0
. Let
\{g_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a sequence of functions mapping from
\mathcal{S}
to
\mathbb{R}
, bounded by
f_{i}\in[a_{i},b_{i}]
respectively. Then when
s_{1}\sim\mu
, for every
\varepsilon>0
, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(s_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(g_{i})>\varepsilon\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1-\lambda}{1+\lambda}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(b_{i}-a_{i})^{2}/4}\right).

The following corollary follows rather directly by combining Theorem
A.21
with Holder’s inequality.

Corollary A.22
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
A.21
, let
\nu
be a distribution on
\mathcal{S}
satisfying Assumption
2.1
with parameters
(p,q)
, then when
s_{1}\sim\nu
, for every
\varepsilon>0
, it can be guaranteed that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(s_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(g_{i})>\varepsilon\right)\leq\left\|\frac{d\mathrm{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}\exp\left(-\frac{1-\lambda}{(1+\lambda)q}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(b_{i}-a_{i})^{2}/4}\right).

Equivalently, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, it can be guaranteed with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}(s_{i})-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu(g_{i})\lesssim\log^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left\|\frac{d\mathrm{\nu}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\right\|_{\mu,p}\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{q}{1-\lambda}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(b_{i}-a_{i})^{2}}.

Appendix B
Proof of theoretical results regarding general Markov chains

B.1
Proof of Theorem
3.5

For simplicity, we firstly consider the case that
\nu=\mu
, and then generalize our result to any distribution
\nu
that satisfies Assumption
2.1
.
A classic Chernoff argument indicates

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{s_{1}\sim\mu}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right\|\geq\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\inf_{t\geq 0}\exp(-nt\varepsilon)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{Tr}\left(\exp\left(t\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\right)\right].

(50)

In order to bound the right-hand-side,
Garg et al. [2018]
illustrated in their Equation (11), through an application of the multi-matrix Golden-Thompson inequality, that there exists a probability distribution
\phi
on the interval
[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}]
, such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}\left(\exp\left(t\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\right)\leq d^{1-\frac{\pi}{4}}\int_{-\frac{\pi}{2}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\prod_{i=n}^{1}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{-\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\right]\mathrm{d}\phi(\theta).

(51)

Furthermore, by repeatedly applying the basic properties of Kronecker products, the trace on the right-hand-side can be computed as

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\prod_{i=n}^{1}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{-\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})]^{\top}\left\{\prod_{i=n}^{1}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{-\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\otimes\prod_{i=n}^{1}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\right\}\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})]^{\top}\prod_{i=n}^{1}\left\{\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{-\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\otimes\exp\left(\frac{2}{\pi}e^{\mathbf{i}\theta}t\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right)\right\}\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})]^{\top}\prod_{i=n}^{1}\exp(t\bm{H}_{i}(s_{i},\theta))\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d}),

(52)

where we define, for simplicity,

\displaystyle\bm{H}_{i}(s_{i},\theta):=\frac{2}{\pi}e^{-\mathbf{i}\theta}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\otimes\bm{I}_{d^{2}}+\bm{I}_{d^{2}}\otimes\frac{2}{\pi}e^{\mathbf{i}\theta}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i}).

(53)

Through the deduction of (
52
), we applied properties (6), (1) and (7) in Theorem
A.4
in the second, third and fourth line respectively.
It is easy to verify that the matrix function
\bm{H}_{i}
defined as in (
53
) has the following properties:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s_{i}\sim\mu}[\bm{H}_{i}(s_{i},\theta)]=\bm{0},\quad\forall i\in[n],\theta\in\left[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right];

(54)

\displaystyle\|\bm{H}_{i}(s_{i},\theta)\|\leq\frac{4}{\pi}M_{i},\quad\text{a.s.},\quad\forall i\in[n],\theta\in\left[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}\right].

(55)

As a combination of (
50
), (
51
) and (
52
), our goal is to bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{F}_{i}(s_{i})\right\|\geq\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2d^{1-\frac{\pi}{4}}\inf_{t\geq 0}\exp(-nt\varepsilon)[\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})]^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim\phi}\mathbb{E}_{s}\left[\prod_{i=n}^{1}\exp(t\bm{H}_{i}(s_{i},\theta))\mathbf{vec}(\bm{I}_{d})\right]
